+
+## Summary
+
+This KEP proposes an option to use the MultiKueue manager as a central scheduler
+across all worker clusters, specifically leveraging Topology Aware Scheduling
+(TAS). In this model, worker clusters act simply as compute pools. The central
+manager handles all admission, preemption, and fair-sharing decisions, and all
+project or team-specific quotas are managed entirely on the manager cluster.
+
+This new centralized scheduling scheme is necessarily more computationally
+expensive than MultiKueue's current mode which primarily manages global quota
+and dispatch and relies on workers to make authoritative scheduling decisions.
+Users will be able to select which mode a MultiKueue instance uses for their
+scheduling and scaling requirements.
+
+## Motivation
+
+Currently, MultiKueue delegates final scheduling decisions to the worker
+clusters. While this works well for many setups, it makes it difficult to
+achieve strict global fair sharing and cross-cluster preemption across an entire
+fleet because the manager lacks visibility into the actual physical capacity and
+topology of the worker nodes.
+
+Allowing the central manager to make these decisions helps organizations that
+manage compute capacity across multiple regions or clouds to better optimize
+their resources. It also simplifies cluster administration by consolidating team
+quota management into a single control plane, rather than needing to manage and
+sync quotas across every worker cluster.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Enable the MultiKueue manager to act as a central scheduler across all worker clusters.
+- Support all workloads currently supported by MultiKueue.
+- Ensure global preemption and fair sharing work seamlessly across the fleet.
+- The manager computes the exact node-level placement (e.g., `kubernetes.io/hostname`) for workloads.
+- Workers execute the manager's placement verbatim without re-evaluating TAS.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Supporting workloads that span multiple clusters (cross-cluster gangs). A
+  single workload must still fit entirely within one worker cluster.
+- Supporting dynamic node provisioning (autoscaling) for TAS workloads in this iteration.
+- Fundamentally change the existing MultiKueue design.
+
+## Proposal
+
+We propose extending MultiKueue to allow the manager to ingest physical node and
+pod inventory from worker clusters into its own scheduler TAS cache. When a
+workload is admitted, the manager will compute the full `TopologyAssignment`
+(treating the worker cluster as the top-level topology domain) and project a
+host-exact placement onto the remote workload. The worker cluster will then skip
+its own scheduling cycle and apply the manager's placement directly.
+
+This solution works because all workloads are already mirrored in the manager
+via MultiKueue. The reason global features like fair-sharing and cross-cluster
+preemption don't work correctly today is simply because the manager lacks
+information about the physical capacity of the clusters and the specific
+topology placement of the workloads. By centralizing the TAS cache across all
+clusters, we give the manager the missing physical context it needs, bringing
+all of these advanced scheduling features alive globally without having to
+rewrite them.
+
+Furthermore, this architectural shift eliminates the need for several complex workarounds currently required in MultiKueue:
+
+- It removes the need for `MultiKueueOrchestratedPreemption` and its associated complexities.
+- It eliminates the need to declare higher, inflated quotas in the manager compared to the workers.
+- It removes the need to maintain a multiplier in the manager quota to account for fleet-wide capacity.
+
+### User Stories (Optional)
+
+#### Story 1: Global Fair Sharing
+
+As a platform administrator managing a fleet of GPU clusters across multiple
+regions, I want to define team quotas centrally on the manager cluster. If Team
+A is under quota and Team B is over quota, I want the manager to ensure Team A
+can preempt Team B's workloads globally, regardless of which specific worker
+cluster Team B's workloads happen to be running on.
+
+#### Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption
+
+As a machine learning engineer, I submit a high-priority distributed training
+job that requires a specific network topology (e.g., all pods on the same rack).
+If the fleet is full, I want the central manager to find the optimal worker
+cluster and preempt exactly the right lower-priority workloads on that specific
+cluster to make room for my job's topology requirements.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+**Risk:** Scalability and performance bottlenecks on the manager cluster.
+**Mitigation:** The manager's TAS cache does not store full pod specs. It only
+caches a highly optimized, pre-aggregated counter of the resources used by
+non-TAS pods per node. This ensures the memory footprint and CPU overhead remain
+minimal even at large scale.
+
+**Risk:** Worker cluster disconnection.
+**Mitigation:** The system relies on MultiKueue's existing `workerLostTimeout`
+mechanism. If a worker disconnects, the manager waits for a grace period. If the
+worker does not reconnect, the manager evicts the workload and retries
+scheduling it on a healthy cluster. When a worker reconnects, the manager
+performs a fresh `List` to rebuild the TAS cache for that cluster.
+
+## Drawbacks
+
+- **Scalability Limits**: Centralizing all node and pod state into a single
+  manager introduces a fundamental scalability limit. While the TAS cache is
+  optimized (storing only aggregated resource counters rather than full pod
+  specs), the manager must still process a high volume of watch events from
+  every worker cluster. However, this architecture should comfortably support
+  the currently documented limits for TAS (100k nodes) and MultiKueue (20 worker
+  clusters).
+- **Distributed System Complexity**: The manager's view of worker capacity is
+  eventually consistent. Network partitions, watch delays, or API server latency
+  on worker clusters can cause the manager to make scheduling decisions based on
+  stale physical capacity data, potentially leading to transient placement
+  failures or race conditions.
+- **Single Point of Failure**: While MultiKueue already relies on the manager
+  for admission, centralizing TAS makes the manager a harder dependency for
+  actual node-level placement. If the manager's TAS cache is degraded, no
+  topology-aware workloads can be scheduled anywhere in the fleet.
+
+## Design Details
+
+### Remote Inventory Sync
+
+The manager will watch `Node` and `Pod` objects from all connected worker
+clusters. These events are fed directly into the manager's scheduler TAS cache. 
+
+To differentiate nodes across clusters, a cluster label (e.g.,
+`kueue.x-k8s.io/multikueue-cluster`) is injected into the remote Node objects as
+they are synced into the cache. This label serves as the top-level topology
+domain for the manager.
+
+The manager reuses the exact same `utiltas.BelongsToNonTASCache` logic as the
+single-cluster controller to track non-TAS usage on the remote nodes.
+
+### Manager-Authoritative Placement
+
+When scheduling a workload, the manager computes the `TopologyAssignment` using
+the worker cluster as the top level and hostname as the lowest level. 
+
+Before stamping the admission onto the remote workload, the manager projects
+this assignment: it strips the cluster level and collapses the assignment to a
+host-exact placement (`Levels: []string{corev1.LabelHostname}`).
+
+### Worker Pass-Through
+
+Workers are configured with a single, massive quota per resource flavor
+(effectively acting as infinite compute pools from a quota perspective). 
+
+When the worker receives the remote workload with the manager-stamped admission,
+it recognizes that the workload is already admitted. The worker skips its own
+scheduling cycle, and its TAS ungater translates the host-exact
```

  I updated the language here to hopefully clarify. Kueue should still perform its normal scheduling, but we want to update the Workload so that the scheduling cycle short circuits only for that Workload. We don't want to affect other Workloads that Kueue happens to be processing around the same time.
  
  Overall, I think the assumption is that the worker clusters will be used exclusively for workloads dispatched by a single MultiKueue manager. @mukund-wayve is that right?

  @mukund-wayve I could also benefit from an additional level of detail in both of these user stories. It would help me if they included what you see Kueue currently do in each of those cases and exactly what you'd rather have it do and why.

```

  IMO, compatible topologies on the worker clusters should be a pre-req verified by whoever is building the clusters, and Kueue shouldn't try to detect or handle that (with this approach). This should result in a similar message as when a workload can't be scheduled in a topology in a single cluster.

@@ -0,0 +1,262 @@
+# KEP-13345: Support centralized scheduling and quota management in MultiKueue
+
+<!-- toc -->
+- [Summary](#summary)
+- [Motivation](#motivation)
+  - [Goals](#goals)
+  - [Non-Goals](#non-goals)
+- [Proposal](#proposal)
+  - [User Stories (Optional)](#user-stories-optional)
+    - [Story 1: Global Fair Sharing](#story-1-global-fair-sharing)
+    - [Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption](#story-2-cross-cluster-preemption)
+  - [Risks and Mitigations](#risks-and-mitigations)
+- [Drawbacks](#drawbacks)
+- [Design Details](#design-details)
+  - [Remote Inventory Sync](#remote-inventory-sync)
+  - [Manager-Authoritative Placement](#manager-authoritative-placement)
+  - [Worker Pass-Through](#worker-pass-through)
+  - [Test Plan](#test-plan)
+  - [Graduation Criteria](#graduation-criteria)
+- [Alternatives](#alternatives)
+<!-- /toc -->
+
+## Summary
+
+This KEP proposes an option to use the MultiKueue manager as a central scheduler
+across all worker clusters, specifically leveraging Topology Aware Scheduling
+(TAS). In this model, worker clusters act simply as compute pools. The central
+manager handles all admission, preemption, and fair-sharing decisions, and all
+project or team-specific quotas are managed entirely on the manager cluster.
+
+This new centralized scheduling scheme is necessarily more computationally
+expensive than MultiKueue's current mode which primarily manages global quota
+and dispatch and relies on workers to make authoritative scheduling decisions.
+Users will be able to select which mode a MultiKueue instance uses for their
+scheduling and scaling requirements.
+
+## Motivation
+
+Currently, MultiKueue delegates final scheduling decisions to the worker
+clusters. While this works well for many setups, it makes it difficult to
+achieve strict global fair sharing and cross-cluster preemption across an entire
+fleet because the manager lacks visibility into the actual physical capacity and
+topology of the worker nodes.
+
+Allowing the central manager to make these decisions helps organizations that
+manage compute capacity across multiple regions or clouds to better optimize
+their resources. It also simplifies cluster administration by consolidating team
+quota management into a single control plane, rather than needing to manage and
+sync quotas across every worker cluster.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Enable the MultiKueue manager to act as a central scheduler across all worker clusters.
+- Support all workloads currently supported by MultiKueue.
+- Ensure global preemption and fair sharing work seamlessly across the fleet.
+- The manager computes the exact node-level placement (e.g., `kubernetes.io/hostname`) for workloads.
+- Workers execute the manager's placement verbatim without re-evaluating TAS.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Supporting workloads that span multiple clusters (cross-cluster gangs). A
+  single workload must still fit entirely within one worker cluster.
+- Supporting dynamic node provisioning (autoscaling) for TAS workloads in this iteration.
+- Fundamentally change the existing MultiKueue design.
+
+## Proposal
+
+We propose extending MultiKueue to allow the manager to ingest physical node and
+pod inventory from worker clusters into its own scheduler TAS cache. When a
+workload is admitted, the manager will compute the full `TopologyAssignment`
+(treating the worker cluster as the top-level topology domain) and project a
+host-exact placement onto the remote workload. The worker cluster will then skip
+the workload during its own scheduling cycle and apply the manager's placement directly.
+
+This solution works because all workloads are already mirrored in the manager
+via MultiKueue. The reason global features like fair-sharing and cross-cluster
+preemption don't work correctly today is simply because the manager lacks
+information about the physical capacity of the clusters and the specific
+topology placement of the workloads. By centralizing the TAS cache across all
+clusters, we give the manager the missing physical context it needs, bringing
+all of these advanced scheduling features alive globally without having to
+rewrite them.
+
+Furthermore, this architectural shift eliminates the need for several complex workarounds currently required in MultiKueue:
+
+- It removes the need for `MultiKueueOrchestratedPreemption` and its associated complexities.
+- It eliminates the need to declare higher, inflated quotas in the manager compared to the workers.
+
+### User Stories (Optional)
+
+#### Story 1: Global Fair Sharing
+
+As a platform administrator managing a fleet of GPU clusters across multiple
+regions, I want to define team quotas centrally on the manager cluster. If Team
+A is under quota and Team B is over quota, I want the manager to ensure Team A
+can preempt Team B's workloads globally, regardless of which specific worker
+cluster Team B's workloads happen to be running on.
+
+#### Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption
+
+As a machine learning engineer, I submit a high-priority distributed training
+job that requires a specific network topology (e.g., all pods on the same rack).
+If the fleet is full, I want the central manager to find the optimal worker
+cluster and preempt exactly the right lower-priority workloads on that specific
+cluster to make room for my job's topology requirements.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+**Risk:** Scalability and performance bottlenecks on the manager cluster.
+
+**Mitigation:** The manager's TAS cache does not store full pod specs. It only
+caches a highly optimized, pre-aggregated counter of the resources used by
+non-TAS pods per node. This ensures the memory footprint and CPU overhead remain
+minimal even at large scale.
+
+**Risk:** Worker cluster disconnection.
+
+**Mitigation:** The system relies on MultiKueue's existing `workerLostTimeout`
+mechanism. If a worker disconnects, the manager waits for a grace period. If the
+worker does not reconnect, the manager evicts the workload and retries
+scheduling it on a healthy cluster. When a worker reconnects, the manager
+performs a fresh `List` to rebuild the TAS cache for that cluster.
+
+## Drawbacks
+
+- **Scalability Limits**: Centralizing all node and pod state into a single
+  manager introduces a fundamental scalability limit. While the TAS cache is
+  optimized (storing only aggregated resource counters rather than full pod
+  specs), the manager must still process a high volume of watch events from
+  every worker cluster. However, this architecture should comfortably support
+  the currently documented limits for TAS (100k nodes) and MultiKueue (20 worker
+  clusters).
+- **Distributed System Complexity**: The manager's view of worker capacity is
+  eventually consistent. Network partitions, watch delays, or API server latency
+  on worker clusters can cause the manager to make scheduling decisions based on
+  stale physical capacity data, potentially leading to transient placement
+  failures or race conditions.
+- **Single Point of Failure**: While MultiKueue already relies on the manager
+  for admission, centralizing TAS makes the manager a harder dependency for
+  actual node-level placement. If the manager's TAS cache is degraded, no
+  topology-aware workloads can be scheduled anywhere in the fleet.
+
+## Design Details
+
+### Configuration
+
+This KEP proposes adding a new mode for MultiKueue and preserving its current
+strategy as the default. The new "centralized" mode necessarily changes the
+scalability profile of the Kueue manager by requiring more data which comes from
+other clusters.
+
+To enable centralized TAS, users must first enable the
+`MultiKueueCentralizedTAS` feature gate in the Kueue manager on the manager
+cluster and each worker cluster. The feature gate allows a new
+`multikueue.centralizedTAS` field to be set in the Kueue Configuration API:
+
+```go
+type MultiKueue struct {
+	...
+
+	// CentralizedTAS configures MultiKueue's "centralized" scheduling scheme.
+	// This field is ignored when the MultiKueueCentralizedTAS feature gate is
+	// disabled.
+	// +optional
+	CentralizedTAS *CentralizedTAS `json:"centralizedTAS,omitempty"`
+}
+
+// CentralizedTAS configures MultiKueue's "centralized" scheduling scheme.
+type CentralizedTAS struct {
+	// Enable controls whether a MultiKueue manager watches the required
+	// resources in each worker cluster. It must be true to allow a ClusterQueue
+	// to be configured to use centralized TAS.
+	// +optional
+	Enable *bool `json:"enable,omitempty"`
+}
+```
+
+The Configuration API changes are intended to allow this functionality to be
+disabled even if the feature gate is graduated to stable and removed.
+
+When the Configuration's `multikueue.centralizedTAS.enabled` is `true`, a
```

  _🗄️ Data Integrity & Integration_ | _🟠 Major_ | _⚡ Quick win_
  
  **Use the API field name `enable`.**
  
  The API in `apis/config/v1beta2/configuration_types.go`, Line 383-387, serializes `CentralizedTAS.Enable` as `centralizedTAS.enable`. Both references here use `enabled`, so following this procedure will not set the field and centralized TAS will remain disabled.
  
  <details>
  <summary>Proposed documentation fix</summary>
  
  ```diff
  - When the Configuration's `multikueue.centralizedTAS.enabled` is `true`
  + When the Configuration's `multikueue.centralizedTAS.enable` is `true`
  
  - `multikueue.centralizedTAS.enabled=true`.
  + `multikueue.centralizedTAS.enable=true`.
  ```
  </details>
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
  Also applies to: 193-193

  only still-valid issues, skip the rest with a brief reason, keep changes
  minimal, and validate.
  
  In `@keps/13345-centralized-multikueue-tas/README.md` at line 181, Update the
  README references to the centralized TAS configuration field from
  multikueue.centralizedTAS.enabled to multikueue.centralizedTAS.enable, including
  the second occurrence, matching the serialized CentralizedTAS.Enable API field.
  ```
  
  </details>

+<!-- /toc -->
+
+## Summary
+

+across all worker clusters, specifically leveraging Topology Aware Scheduling
+(TAS). In this model, worker clusters act simply as compute pools. The central

+expensive than MultiKueue's current mode which primarily manages global quota
+and dispatch and relies on workers to make authoritative scheduling decisions.
+Users will be able to select which mode a MultiKueue instance uses for their
+scheduling and scaling requirements.
+

+
+Currently, MultiKueue delegates final scheduling decisions to the worker
+clusters. While this works well for many setups, it makes it difficult to
+achieve strict global fair sharing and cross-cluster preemption across an entire
+fleet because the manager lacks visibility into the actual physical capacity and
+topology of the worker nodes.
+
+Allowing the central manager to make these decisions helps organizations that
+manage compute capacity across multiple regions or clouds to better optimize
+their resources. It also simplifies cluster administration by consolidating team
+quota management into a single control plane, rather than needing to manage and
+sync quotas across every worker cluster.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Enable the MultiKueue manager to act as a central scheduler across all worker clusters.
+- Support all workloads currently supported by MultiKueue.
+- Ensure global preemption and fair sharing work seamlessly across the fleet.
+- The manager computes the exact node-level placement (e.g., `kubernetes.io/hostname`) for workloads.
+- Workers execute the manager's placement verbatim without re-evaluating TAS.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Supporting workloads that span multiple clusters (cross-cluster gangs). A
+  single workload must still fit entirely within one worker cluster.
+- Supporting dynamic node provisioning (autoscaling) for TAS workloads in this iteration.
+- Fundamentally change the existing MultiKueue design.
+
+## Proposal
+
+We propose extending MultiKueue to allow the manager to ingest physical node and
+pod inventory from worker clusters into its own scheduler TAS cache. When a
+workload is admitted, the manager will compute the full `TopologyAssignment`
+(treating the worker cluster as the top-level topology domain) and project a

+its own scheduling cycle and apply the manager's placement directly.
+
+This solution works because all workloads are already mirrored in the manager
+via MultiKueue. The reason global features like fair-sharing and cross-cluster
+preemption don't work correctly today is simply because the manager lacks
+information about the physical capacity of the clusters and the specific
+topology placement of the workloads. By centralizing the TAS cache across all
+clusters, we give the manager the missing physical context it needs, bringing
+all of these advanced scheduling features alive globally without having to
+rewrite them.
+
+Furthermore, this architectural shift eliminates the need for several complex workarounds currently required in MultiKueue:
+
+- It removes the need for `MultiKueueOrchestratedPreemption` and its associated complexities.
+- It eliminates the need to declare higher, inflated quotas in the manager compared to the workers.
+- It removes the need to maintain a multiplier in the manager quota to account for fleet-wide capacity.
+
+### User Stories (Optional)
+
+#### Story 1: Global Fair Sharing
+
+As a platform administrator managing a fleet of GPU clusters across multiple
+regions, I want to define team quotas centrally on the manager cluster. If Team
+A is under quota and Team B is over quota, I want the manager to ensure Team A
+can preempt Team B's workloads globally, regardless of which specific worker
+cluster Team B's workloads happen to be running on.
+
+#### Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption
```

  Sorry for the confusion. I agree that this is not cross-cluster preemption. We still want preemptions to be localised within each worker cluster.
  
  Both stories point to the same fundamental behaviour: global quotas and fair sharing, but from different user perspectives.
  
  We could probably replace user story 2 with one that highlights the operational benefit:
  
  ### Story 2: Operational ease
  
  As a platform administrator, I can define quotas for all worker clusters in the manager cluster
  without having to decide how those quotas should be distributed between individual clusters.
  
  Currently, the administrator must manually determine this distribution,
  which is operationally difficult and can lead to suboptimal workload placement.
  
  #### Before
  
  | Team | Manager | Worker 1 | Worker 2 |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Team A | 16 GPUs | 8 GPUs | 8 GPUs |
  | Team B | 10 GPUs | 1 GPU | 9 GPUs |
  | Team C | 6 GPUs | 1 GPU | 5 GPUs |
  
  #### After
  
  | Team | Manager | Worker 1 | Worker 2 |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Team A | 16 GPUs | 10000 (arbitrarily high) | 10000 (arbitrarily high) |
  | Team B | 10 GPUs | 10000 (arbitrarily high) | 10000 (arbitrarily high) |
  | Team C | 6 GPUs | 10000 (arbitrarily high) | 10000 (arbitrarily high) |

+
+This solution works because all workloads are already mirrored in the manager
+via MultiKueue. The reason global features like fair-sharing and cross-cluster
+preemption don't work correctly today is simply because the manager lacks
+information about the physical capacity of the clusters and the specific
+topology placement of the workloads. By centralizing the TAS cache across all
+clusters, we give the manager the missing physical context it needs, bringing
+all of these advanced scheduling features alive globally without having to
+rewrite them.
+
+Furthermore, this architectural shift eliminates the need for several complex workarounds currently required in MultiKueue:
+
+- It removes the need for `MultiKueueOrchestratedPreemption` and its associated complexities.
+- It eliminates the need to declare higher, inflated quotas in the manager compared to the workers.
+- It removes the need to maintain a multiplier in the manager quota to account for fleet-wide capacity.
+
+### User Stories (Optional)
+
+#### Story 1: Global Fair Sharing
+
+As a platform administrator managing a fleet of GPU clusters across multiple
+regions, I want to define team quotas centrally on the manager cluster. If Team
+A is under quota and Team B is over quota, I want the manager to ensure Team A
+can preempt Team B's workloads globally, regardless of which specific worker
+cluster Team B's workloads happen to be running on.
+
+#### Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption
+
+As a machine learning engineer, I submit a high-priority distributed training
+job that requires a specific network topology (e.g., all pods on the same rack).
+If the fleet is full, I want the central manager to find the optimal worker
+cluster and preempt exactly the right lower-priority workloads on that specific
+cluster to make room for my job's topology requirements.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+**Risk:** Scalability and performance bottlenecks on the manager cluster.
+**Mitigation:** The manager's TAS cache does not store full pod specs. It only
+caches a highly optimized, pre-aggregated counter of the resources used by
+non-TAS pods per node. This ensures the memory footprint and CPU overhead remain
+minimal even at large scale.
+
+**Risk:** Worker cluster disconnection.
+**Mitigation:** The system relies on MultiKueue's existing `workerLostTimeout`
+mechanism. If a worker disconnects, the manager waits for a grace period. If the
+worker does not reconnect, the manager evicts the workload and retries
+scheduling it on a healthy cluster. When a worker reconnects, the manager
+performs a fresh `List` to rebuild the TAS cache for that cluster.
+
+## Drawbacks
+
+- **Scalability Limits**: Centralizing all node and pod state into a single
+  manager introduces a fundamental scalability limit. While the TAS cache is
+  optimized (storing only aggregated resource counters rather than full pod
+  specs), the manager must still process a high volume of watch events from
+  every worker cluster. However, this architecture should comfortably support
+  the currently documented limits for TAS (100k nodes) and MultiKueue (20 worker
+  clusters).
+- **Distributed System Complexity**: The manager's view of worker capacity is
+  eventually consistent. Network partitions, watch delays, or API server latency
+  on worker clusters can cause the manager to make scheduling decisions based on
+  stale physical capacity data, potentially leading to transient placement
+  failures or race conditions.
+- **Single Point of Failure**: While MultiKueue already relies on the manager
+  for admission, centralizing TAS makes the manager a harder dependency for
+  actual node-level placement. If the manager's TAS cache is degraded, no
+  topology-aware workloads can be scheduled anywhere in the fleet.
+
+## Design Details
+
+### Remote Inventory Sync
+
+The manager will watch `Node` and `Pod` objects from all connected worker
+clusters. These events are fed directly into the manager's scheduler TAS cache. 
+
+To differentiate nodes across clusters, a cluster label (e.g.,
+`kueue.x-k8s.io/multikueue-cluster`) is injected into the remote Node objects as
```

@@ -0,0 +1,205 @@
+# KEP-13345: Support centralized scheduling and quota management in MultiKueue
+
+<!-- toc -->
+- [Summary](#summary)
+- [Motivation](#motivation)
+  - [Goals](#goals)
+  - [Non-Goals](#non-goals)
+- [Proposal](#proposal)
+  - [User Stories (Optional)](#user-stories-optional)
+    - [Story 1: Global Fair Sharing](#story-1-global-fair-sharing)
+    - [Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption](#story-2-cross-cluster-preemption)
+  - [Risks and Mitigations](#risks-and-mitigations)
+- [Drawbacks](#drawbacks)
+- [Design Details](#design-details)
+  - [Remote Inventory Sync](#remote-inventory-sync)
+  - [Manager-Authoritative Placement](#manager-authoritative-placement)
+  - [Worker Pass-Through](#worker-pass-through)
+  - [Test Plan](#test-plan)
+  - [Graduation Criteria](#graduation-criteria)
+- [Alternatives](#alternatives)
+<!-- /toc -->
+
+## Summary
+
+This KEP proposes an option to use the MultiKueue manager as a central scheduler
+across all worker clusters, specifically leveraging Topology Aware Scheduling
+(TAS). In this model, worker clusters act simply as compute pools. The central
+manager handles all admission, preemption, and fair-sharing decisions, and all
+project or team-specific quotas are managed entirely on the manager cluster.
+
+This new centralized scheduling scheme is necessarily more computationally
+expensive than MultiKueue's current mode which primarily manages global quota
+and dispatch and relies on workers to make authoritative scheduling decisions.
+Users will be able to select which mode a MultiKueue instance uses for their
+scheduling and scaling requirements.
+
+## Motivation
+
+Currently, MultiKueue delegates final scheduling decisions to the worker
+clusters. While this works well for many setups, it makes it difficult to
+achieve strict global fair sharing and cross-cluster preemption across an entire
+fleet because the manager lacks visibility into the actual physical capacity and
+topology of the worker nodes.
+
+Allowing the central manager to make these decisions helps organizations that
+manage compute capacity across multiple regions or clouds to better optimize
+their resources. It also simplifies cluster administration by consolidating team
+quota management into a single control plane, rather than needing to manage and
+sync quotas across every worker cluster.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Enable the MultiKueue manager to act as a central scheduler across all worker clusters.
+- Support all workloads currently supported by MultiKueue.
+- Ensure global preemption and fair sharing work seamlessly across the fleet.
+- The manager computes the exact node-level placement (e.g., `kubernetes.io/hostname`) for workloads.
+- Workers execute the manager's placement verbatim without re-evaluating TAS.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Supporting workloads that span multiple clusters (cross-cluster gangs). A
+  single workload must still fit entirely within one worker cluster.
+- Supporting dynamic node provisioning (autoscaling) for TAS workloads in this iteration.
+- Fundamentally change the existing MultiKueue design.
+
+## Proposal
+
+We propose extending MultiKueue to allow the manager to ingest physical node and
+pod inventory from worker clusters into its own scheduler TAS cache. When a
+workload is admitted, the manager will compute the full `TopologyAssignment`
+(treating the worker cluster as the top-level topology domain) and project a
+host-exact placement onto the remote workload. The worker cluster will then skip
+its own scheduling cycle and apply the manager's placement directly.
+
+This solution works because all workloads are already mirrored in the manager
+via MultiKueue. The reason global features like fair-sharing and cross-cluster
+preemption don't work correctly today is simply because the manager lacks
+information about the physical capacity of the clusters and the specific
+topology placement of the workloads. By centralizing the TAS cache across all
+clusters, we give the manager the missing physical context it needs, bringing
+all of these advanced scheduling features alive globally without having to
+rewrite them.
+
+Furthermore, this architectural shift eliminates the need for several complex workarounds currently required in MultiKueue:
+
+- It removes the need for `MultiKueueOrchestratedPreemption` and its associated complexities.
+- It eliminates the need to declare higher, inflated quotas in the manager compared to the workers.
+- It removes the need to maintain a multiplier in the manager quota to account for fleet-wide capacity.
+
+### User Stories (Optional)
+
+#### Story 1: Global Fair Sharing
+
+As a platform administrator managing a fleet of GPU clusters across multiple
+regions, I want to define team quotas centrally on the manager cluster. If Team
+A is under quota and Team B is over quota, I want the manager to ensure Team A
+can preempt Team B's workloads globally, regardless of which specific worker
+cluster Team B's workloads happen to be running on.
+
+#### Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption
+
+As a machine learning engineer, I submit a high-priority distributed training
+job that requires a specific network topology (e.g., all pods on the same rack).
+If the fleet is full, I want the central manager to find the optimal worker
+cluster and preempt exactly the right lower-priority workloads on that specific
+cluster to make room for my job's topology requirements.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+**Risk:** Scalability and performance bottlenecks on the manager cluster.
+**Mitigation:** The manager's TAS cache does not store full pod specs. It only
+caches a highly optimized, pre-aggregated counter of the resources used by
+non-TAS pods per node. This ensures the memory footprint and CPU overhead remain
+minimal even at large scale.
+
+**Risk:** Worker cluster disconnection.
+**Mitigation:** The system relies on MultiKueue's existing `workerLostTimeout`
+mechanism. If a worker disconnects, the manager waits for a grace period. If the
+worker does not reconnect, the manager evicts the workload and retries
+scheduling it on a healthy cluster. When a worker reconnects, the manager
+performs a fresh `List` to rebuild the TAS cache for that cluster.
```

  Yes. That seems like a sensible thing to do. Is this the same behaviour as the current MultiKueue setup?

+

+topology placement of the workloads. By centralizing the TAS cache across all

+
+Furthermore, this architectural shift eliminates the need for several complex workarounds currently required in MultiKueue:
+
+- It removes the need for `MultiKueueOrchestratedPreemption` and its associated complexities.
+- It eliminates the need to declare higher, inflated quotas in the manager compared to the workers.
+- It removes the need to maintain a multiplier in the manager quota to account for fleet-wide capacity.
+
+### User Stories (Optional)
+
+#### Story 1: Global Fair Sharing
+
+As a platform administrator managing a fleet of GPU clusters across multiple
+regions, I want to define team quotas centrally on the manager cluster. If Team
+A is under quota and Team B is over quota, I want the manager to ensure Team A

```

  > BTW, another question: will the manager be able to decide to preempt a Kueue-but-non-Multi-Kueue workload on a worker?
  
  No. I think it's best to have the manager only preempt workloads it admitted and more generally, have disjoint set of nodes managed by centralised-tas and other CQs. 
  
  We can naturally enforce this by validating that all active CQs (no stopPolicy set on CQ) that share a topology either are all managed by centralised-tas or by non-centralised-tas and never a mix. Then the manager does not have to preempt local Kueue jobs and the scheduling responsibilities stay clean and easy to follow. This means that Local CQs, classic MultiKueue and centralised MultiKueue can still co-exist. 
  
  This could be challenging during a migration though. For that, we can use the `Hold` or `HoldAndDrain` stopPolocy feature on a CQ so that we don't allow new admissions on legacy CQs allowing to cleanly migrate to the centralised-tas mode.
  
  For example:
  ```mermaid
  flowchart LR
    subgraph okCentral [Valid - all centralized]
      C1[CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED]
      C2[CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED]
      TC[Topology_CENTRALISED]
      C1 --> TC
      C2 --> TC
    end
  
    subgraph okClassic [Valid - all not centralized]
      N1[CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED]
      N2[CQ_W2]
      TN[Topology_NON_CENTRALISED]
      N1 --> TN
      N2 --> TN
    end
  
    subgraph badMix [Invalid - mix]
      M1[CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED]
      M2[CQ_W1]
      TM[Topology_CENTRALISED]
      M1 --> TM
      M2 --> TM
    end
  
  ```
  
  
  Below is a valid configuration:
  
  ```mermaid
  flowchart LR
    subgraph manager [Manager]
      CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED
      CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED
    end
  
    subgraph w1 [Worker w1]
      CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED_w1[CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED]
      Topology_CENTRALISED_w1[Topology_CENTRALISED]
      CQ_W1
      Topology_W1
    end
  
    subgraph w2 [Worker w2]
      CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED_w2[CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED]
      Topology_CENTRALISED_w2[Topology_CENTRALISED]
      CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED_w2[CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED]
      CQ_W2
      Topology_NON_CENTRALISED_w2[Topology_NON_CENTRALISED]
    end
  
    subgraph w3 [Worker w3]
      CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED_w3[CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED]
      Topology_NON_CENTRALISED_w3[Topology_NON_CENTRALISED]
    end
  
    CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED --> CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED_w1
    CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED --> CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED_w2
    CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED --> CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED_w2
    CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED --> CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED_w3
    CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED_w1 --> Topology_CENTRALISED_w1
    CQ_W1 --> Topology_W1
    CQ_SHARED_CENTRALISED_w2 --> Topology_CENTRALISED_w2
    CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED_w2 --> Topology_NON_CENTRALISED_w2
    CQ_W2 --> Topology_NON_CENTRALISED_w2
    CQ_SHARED_NON_CENTRALISED_w3 --> Topology_NON_CENTRALISED_w3
  
  ```
  
  Example of how a migration could look:
  Dotted lines indicate that the CQs are stopped using a `stopPolicy`
  
  ```mermaid
  flowchart LR
    subgraph manager [Manager]
      CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED
      CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED
      CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED
      CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED
    end
  
    subgraph w1 [Worker w1]
      CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED_w1[CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED]
      CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED_w1[CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED]
      CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED_w1[CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED]
      CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED_w1[CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED]
      Topology_w1[Topology]
    end
  
    subgraph w2 [Worker w2]
      CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED_w2[CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED]
      CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED_w2[CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED]
      CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED_w2[CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED]
      CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED_w2[CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED]
      Topology_w2[Topology]
    end
  
    CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED --> CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED_w1
    CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED --> CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED_w2
    CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED --> CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED_w1
    CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED --> CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED_w2
    CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED -.-> CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED_w1
    CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED -.-> CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED_w2
    CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED -.-> CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED_w1
    CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED -.-> CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED_w2
    CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED_w1 --> Topology_w1
    CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED_w1 --> Topology_w1
    CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED_w1 -.-> Topology_w1
    CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED_w1 -.-> Topology_w1
    CQ_TEAM_A_CENTRALISED_w2 --> Topology_w2
    CQ_TEAM_B_CENTRALISED_w2 --> Topology_w2
    CQ_TEAM_A_NON_CENTRALISED_w2 -.-> Topology_w2
    CQ_TEAM_B_NON_CENTRALISED_w2 -.-> Topology_w2
  
  ```

@@ -0,0 +1,262 @@
+# KEP-13345: Support centralized scheduling and quota management in MultiKueue
+
+<!-- toc -->
+- [Summary](#summary)
+- [Motivation](#motivation)
+  - [Goals](#goals)
+  - [Non-Goals](#non-goals)
+- [Proposal](#proposal)
+  - [User Stories (Optional)](#user-stories-optional)
+    - [Story 1: Global Fair Sharing](#story-1-global-fair-sharing)
+    - [Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption](#story-2-cross-cluster-preemption)
+  - [Risks and Mitigations](#risks-and-mitigations)
+- [Drawbacks](#drawbacks)
+- [Design Details](#design-details)
+  - [Remote Inventory Sync](#remote-inventory-sync)
+  - [Manager-Authoritative Placement](#manager-authoritative-placement)
+  - [Worker Pass-Through](#worker-pass-through)
+  - [Test Plan](#test-plan)
+  - [Graduation Criteria](#graduation-criteria)
+- [Alternatives](#alternatives)
+<!-- /toc -->
+
+## Summary
+
+This KEP proposes an option to use the MultiKueue manager as a central scheduler
+across all worker clusters, specifically leveraging Topology Aware Scheduling
+(TAS). In this model, worker clusters act simply as compute pools. The central
+manager handles all admission, preemption, and fair-sharing decisions, and all
+project or team-specific quotas are managed entirely on the manager cluster.
+
+This new centralized scheduling scheme is necessarily more computationally
+expensive than MultiKueue's current mode which primarily manages global quota
+and dispatch and relies on workers to make authoritative scheduling decisions.
+Users will be able to select which mode a MultiKueue instance uses for their
+scheduling and scaling requirements.
+
+## Motivation
+
+Currently, MultiKueue delegates final scheduling decisions to the worker
+clusters. While this works well for many setups, it makes it difficult to
+achieve strict global fair sharing and cross-cluster preemption across an entire
+fleet because the manager lacks visibility into the actual physical capacity and
+topology of the worker nodes.
+
+Allowing the central manager to make these decisions helps organizations that
+manage compute capacity across multiple regions or clouds to better optimize
+their resources. It also simplifies cluster administration by consolidating team
+quota management into a single control plane, rather than needing to manage and
+sync quotas across every worker cluster.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Enable the MultiKueue manager to act as a central scheduler across all worker clusters.
+- Support all workloads currently supported by MultiKueue.
+- Ensure global preemption and fair sharing work seamlessly across the fleet.
+- The manager computes the exact node-level placement (e.g., `kubernetes.io/hostname`) for workloads.
+- Workers execute the manager's placement verbatim without re-evaluating TAS.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Supporting workloads that span multiple clusters (cross-cluster gangs). A
+  single workload must still fit entirely within one worker cluster.
+- Supporting dynamic node provisioning (autoscaling) for TAS workloads in this iteration.
+- Fundamentally change the existing MultiKueue design.
+
+## Proposal
+
+We propose extending MultiKueue to allow the manager to ingest physical node and
+pod inventory from worker clusters into its own scheduler TAS cache. When a
+workload is admitted, the manager will compute the full `TopologyAssignment`
+(treating the worker cluster as the top-level topology domain) and project a
+host-exact placement onto the remote workload. The worker cluster will then skip
+the workload during its own scheduling cycle and apply the manager's placement directly.
+
+This solution works because all workloads are already mirrored in the manager
+via MultiKueue. The reason global features like fair-sharing and cross-cluster
+preemption don't work correctly today is simply because the manager lacks
+information about the physical capacity of the clusters and the specific
+topology placement of the workloads. By centralizing the TAS cache across all
+clusters, we give the manager the missing physical context it needs, bringing
+all of these advanced scheduling features alive globally without having to
+rewrite them.
+
+Furthermore, this architectural shift eliminates the need for several complex workarounds currently required in MultiKueue:
+
+- It removes the need for `MultiKueueOrchestratedPreemption` and its associated complexities.
+- It eliminates the need to declare higher, inflated quotas in the manager compared to the workers.
+
+### User Stories (Optional)
+
+#### Story 1: Global Fair Sharing
+
+As a platform administrator managing a fleet of GPU clusters across multiple
+regions, I want to define team quotas centrally on the manager cluster. If Team
+A is under quota and Team B is over quota, I want the manager to ensure Team A
+can preempt Team B's workloads globally, regardless of which specific worker
+cluster Team B's workloads happen to be running on.
+
+#### Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption
+
+As a machine learning engineer, I submit a high-priority distributed training
+job that requires a specific network topology (e.g., all pods on the same rack).
+If the fleet is full, I want the central manager to find the optimal worker
+cluster and preempt exactly the right lower-priority workloads on that specific
+cluster to make room for my job's topology requirements.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+**Risk:** Scalability and performance bottlenecks on the manager cluster.
```

  Should we highlight the possibility of aggregation drift between worker and manager states? Or would we guarantee correctness somehow? Mitigation could be a periodic reconciler?

  > > by whoever is building the clusters

  > @nojnhuh What do you mean by this?
  
  If two worker clusters have inverse definitions for some "block" and "rack" Node labels, then I think that should be reconciled before we try to get MultiKueue to schedule workloads based on those labels. I don't think it should be Kueue's job to try to detect or resolve that since other cluster components might be similarly confused.

@@ -0,0 +1,263 @@
+# KEP-13345: Support centralized scheduling and quota management in MultiKueue
+
+<!-- toc -->
+- [Summary](#summary)
+- [Motivation](#motivation)
+  - [Goals](#goals)
+  - [Non-Goals](#non-goals)
+- [Proposal](#proposal)
+  - [User Stories (Optional)](#user-stories-optional)
+    - [Story 1: Global Fair Sharing](#story-1-global-fair-sharing)
+    - [Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption](#story-2-cross-cluster-preemption)
+  - [Risks and Mitigations](#risks-and-mitigations)
+- [Drawbacks](#drawbacks)
+- [Design Details](#design-details)
+  - [Configuration](#configuration)
+  - [Remote Inventory Sync](#remote-inventory-sync)
+  - [Manager-Authoritative Placement](#manager-authoritative-placement)
+  - [Worker Pass-Through](#worker-pass-through)
+  - [Test Plan](#test-plan)
+  - [Graduation Criteria](#graduation-criteria)
+- [Alternatives](#alternatives)
+<!-- /toc -->
+
+## Summary
+
+This KEP proposes an option to use the MultiKueue manager as a central scheduler
+across all worker clusters, specifically leveraging Topology Aware Scheduling
+(TAS). In this model, worker clusters act simply as compute pools. The central
+manager handles all admission, preemption, and fair-sharing decisions, and all
+project or team-specific quotas are managed entirely on the manager cluster.
+
+This new centralized scheduling scheme is necessarily more computationally
+expensive than MultiKueue's current mode which primarily manages global quota
+and dispatch and relies on workers to make authoritative scheduling decisions.
+Users will be able to select which mode a MultiKueue instance uses for their
+scheduling and scaling requirements.
+
+## Motivation
+
+Currently, MultiKueue delegates final scheduling decisions to the worker
+clusters. While this works well for many setups, it makes it difficult to
+achieve strict global fair sharing and cross-cluster preemption across an entire
+fleet because the manager lacks visibility into the actual physical capacity and
+topology of the worker nodes.
+
+Allowing the central manager to make these decisions helps organizations that
+manage compute capacity across multiple regions or clouds to better optimize
+their resources. It also simplifies cluster administration by consolidating team
+quota management into a single control plane, rather than needing to manage and
+sync quotas across every worker cluster.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Enable the MultiKueue manager to act as a central scheduler across all worker clusters.
+- Support all workloads currently supported by MultiKueue.
+- Ensure global preemption and fair sharing work seamlessly across the fleet.
+- The manager computes the exact node-level placement (e.g., `kubernetes.io/hostname`) for workloads.
+- Workers execute the manager's placement verbatim without re-evaluating TAS.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Supporting workloads that span multiple clusters (cross-cluster gangs). A
+  single workload must still fit entirely within one worker cluster.
+- Supporting dynamic node provisioning (autoscaling) for TAS workloads in this iteration.
+- Fundamentally change the existing MultiKueue design.
+
+## Proposal
+
+We propose extending MultiKueue to allow the manager to ingest physical node and
+pod inventory from worker clusters into its own scheduler TAS cache. When a
+workload is admitted, the manager will compute the full `TopologyAssignment`
+(treating the worker cluster as the top-level topology domain) and project a
+host-exact placement onto the remote workload. The worker cluster will then skip
+the workload during its own scheduling cycle and apply the manager's placement directly.
+
+This solution works because all workloads are already mirrored in the manager
+via MultiKueue. The reason global features like fair-sharing and cross-cluster
+preemption don't work correctly today is simply because the manager lacks
+information about the physical capacity of the clusters and the specific
+topology placement of the workloads. By centralizing the TAS cache across all
+clusters, we give the manager the missing physical context it needs, bringing
+all of these advanced scheduling features alive globally without having to
+rewrite them.
+
+Furthermore, this architectural shift eliminates the need for several complex workarounds currently required in MultiKueue:
+
+- It removes the need for `MultiKueueOrchestratedPreemption` and its associated complexities.
+- It eliminates the need to declare higher, inflated quotas in the manager compared to the workers.
+
+### User Stories (Optional)
+
+#### Story 1: Global Fair Sharing
+
+As a platform administrator managing a fleet of GPU clusters across multiple
+regions, I want to define team quotas centrally on the manager cluster. If Team
+A is under quota and Team B is over quota, I want the manager to ensure Team A
+can preempt Team B's workloads globally, regardless of which specific worker
+cluster Team B's workloads happen to be running on.
+
+#### Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption
+
+As a machine learning engineer, I submit a high-priority distributed training
+job that requires a specific network topology (e.g., all pods on the same rack).
+If the fleet is full, I want the central manager to find the optimal worker
+cluster and preempt exactly the right lower-priority workloads on that specific
+cluster to make room for my job's topology requirements.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+**Risk:** Scalability and performance bottlenecks on the manager cluster.
+
+**Mitigation:** The manager's TAS cache does not store full pod specs. It only
+caches a highly optimized, pre-aggregated counter of the resources used by
+non-TAS pods per node. This ensures the memory footprint and CPU overhead remain
+minimal even at large scale.
+
+**Risk:** Worker cluster disconnection.
+
+**Mitigation:** The system relies on MultiKueue's existing `workerLostTimeout`
+mechanism. If a worker disconnects, the manager waits for a grace period. If the
+worker does not reconnect, the manager evicts the workload and retries
+scheduling it on a healthy cluster. When a worker reconnects, the manager
+performs a fresh `List` to rebuild the TAS cache for that cluster.
+
+## Drawbacks
+
+- **Scalability Limits**: Centralizing all node and pod state into a single
+  manager introduces a fundamental scalability limit. While the TAS cache is
+  optimized (storing only aggregated resource counters rather than full pod
+  specs), the manager must still process a high volume of watch events from
+  every worker cluster. However, this architecture should comfortably support
+  the currently documented limits for TAS (100k nodes) and MultiKueue (20 worker
+  clusters).
+- **Distributed System Complexity**: The manager's view of worker capacity is
+  eventually consistent. Network partitions, watch delays, or API server latency
+  on worker clusters can cause the manager to make scheduling decisions based on
+  stale physical capacity data, potentially leading to transient placement
+  failures or race conditions.
+- **Single Point of Failure**: While MultiKueue already relies on the manager
+  for admission, centralizing TAS makes the manager a harder dependency for
+  actual node-level placement. If the manager's TAS cache is degraded, no
+  topology-aware workloads can be scheduled anywhere in the fleet.
+
+## Design Details
+
+### Configuration
+
+This KEP proposes adding a new mode for MultiKueue and preserving its current
+strategy as the default. The new "centralized" mode necessarily changes the
+scalability profile of the Kueue manager by requiring more data which comes from
+other clusters.
+
+To enable centralized TAS, users must first enable the
+`MultiKueueCentralizedTAS` feature gate in the Kueue manager on the manager
+cluster and each worker cluster. The feature gate allows a new
+`multikueue.centralizedTAS` field to be set in the Kueue Configuration API:
+
+```go
+type MultiKueue struct {
+	...
+
+	// CentralizedTAS configures MultiKueue's "centralized" scheduling scheme.
+	// This field is ignored when the MultiKueueCentralizedTAS feature gate is
+	// disabled.
+	// +optional
+	CentralizedTAS *CentralizedTAS `json:"centralizedTAS,omitempty"`
+}
+
+// CentralizedTAS configures MultiKueue's "centralized" scheduling scheme.
+type CentralizedTAS struct {
+	// Enable controls whether a MultiKueue manager watches the required
+	// resources in each worker cluster. It must be true to allow a ClusterQueue
+	// to be configured to use centralized TAS.
+	// +optional
+	Enable *bool `json:"enable,omitempty"`
+}
+```
+
+The Configuration API changes are intended to allow this functionality to be
+disabled even if the feature gate is graduated to stable and removed.
+
+When the Configuration's `multikueue.centralizedTAS.enable` is `true`, a
+ClusterQueue can be configured to use centralized TAS with the
+`kueue.x-k8s.io/multikueue-centralized-tas="true"` annotation in addition to a
```

  I'm guessing there's a better way to distinguish centralized TAS ClusterQueues from regular MultiKueue ones. Is the MultiKueueConfig a better place to record this? Following [where TAS is currently delayed for MultiKueue](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/kueue/blob/00bbe7fdae401084a764b01e17787731799d5686/pkg/scheduler/flavorassigner/tas_flavorassigner.go#L105), `cq.HasMultiKueueAdmissionCheck()` is derived only from the ClusterQueue. Is fetching the AdmissionCheck and then the MultiKueueConfig more than we'd want to do in an event handler (for fear of blocking other events)?

+- The manager computes the exact node-level placement (e.g., `kubernetes.io/hostname`) for workloads.
+- Workers execute the manager's placement verbatim without re-evaluating TAS.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Supporting workloads that span multiple clusters (cross-cluster gangs). A
+  single workload must still fit entirely within one worker cluster.
+- Supporting dynamic node provisioning (autoscaling) for TAS workloads in this iteration.
+- Fundamentally change the existing MultiKueue design.

```

  As we just discussed on the wg-batch meeting, maybe we can afford watching the `Topology` resources on all the workers, and reconciling their updates on the manager.
  
  I'm assuming `Topology` is not very heavy, nor very numerous per cluster.
  
  And then, we wouldn't prevent worker Topology changes - but could e.g. deactivate the manager ClusterQueue once our assumptions get invalid.

@@ -0,0 +1,205 @@
+# KEP-13345: Support centralized scheduling and quota management in MultiKueue
+
+<!-- toc -->
+- [Summary](#summary)
+- [Motivation](#motivation)
+  - [Goals](#goals)
+  - [Non-Goals](#non-goals)
+- [Proposal](#proposal)
+  - [User Stories (Optional)](#user-stories-optional)
+    - [Story 1: Global Fair Sharing](#story-1-global-fair-sharing)
+    - [Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption](#story-2-cross-cluster-preemption)
+  - [Risks and Mitigations](#risks-and-mitigations)
+- [Drawbacks](#drawbacks)
+- [Design Details](#design-details)
+  - [Remote Inventory Sync](#remote-inventory-sync)
+  - [Manager-Authoritative Placement](#manager-authoritative-placement)
+  - [Worker Pass-Through](#worker-pass-through)
+  - [Test Plan](#test-plan)
+  - [Graduation Criteria](#graduation-criteria)
+- [Alternatives](#alternatives)
+<!-- /toc -->
+
+## Summary
+
+This KEP proposes an option to use the MultiKueue manager as a central scheduler
+across all worker clusters, specifically leveraging Topology Aware Scheduling
+(TAS). In this model, worker clusters act simply as compute pools. The central
+manager handles all admission, preemption, and fair-sharing decisions, and all
+project or team-specific quotas are managed entirely on the manager cluster.
+
+This new centralized scheduling scheme is necessarily more computationally
+expensive than MultiKueue's current mode which primarily manages global quota
+and dispatch and relies on workers to make authoritative scheduling decisions.
+Users will be able to select which mode a MultiKueue instance uses for their
+scheduling and scaling requirements.
+
+## Motivation
+
+Currently, MultiKueue delegates final scheduling decisions to the worker
+clusters. While this works well for many setups, it makes it difficult to
+achieve strict global fair sharing and cross-cluster preemption across an entire
+fleet because the manager lacks visibility into the actual physical capacity and
+topology of the worker nodes.
+
+Allowing the central manager to make these decisions helps organizations that
+manage compute capacity across multiple regions or clouds to better optimize
+their resources. It also simplifies cluster administration by consolidating team
+quota management into a single control plane, rather than needing to manage and
+sync quotas across every worker cluster.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Enable the MultiKueue manager to act as a central scheduler across all worker clusters.
+- Support all workloads currently supported by MultiKueue.
+- Ensure global preemption and fair sharing work seamlessly across the fleet.
+- The manager computes the exact node-level placement (e.g., `kubernetes.io/hostname`) for workloads.
+- Workers execute the manager's placement verbatim without re-evaluating TAS.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Supporting workloads that span multiple clusters (cross-cluster gangs). A
+  single workload must still fit entirely within one worker cluster.
+- Supporting dynamic node provisioning (autoscaling) for TAS workloads in this iteration.
+- Fundamentally change the existing MultiKueue design.
+
+## Proposal
+
+We propose extending MultiKueue to allow the manager to ingest physical node and
+pod inventory from worker clusters into its own scheduler TAS cache. When a
+workload is admitted, the manager will compute the full `TopologyAssignment`
+(treating the worker cluster as the top-level topology domain) and project a
+host-exact placement onto the remote workload. The worker cluster will then skip
+its own scheduling cycle and apply the manager's placement directly.
+
+This solution works because all workloads are already mirrored in the manager
+via MultiKueue. The reason global features like fair-sharing and cross-cluster
+preemption don't work correctly today is simply because the manager lacks
+information about the physical capacity of the clusters and the specific
+topology placement of the workloads. By centralizing the TAS cache across all
+clusters, we give the manager the missing physical context it needs, bringing
+all of these advanced scheduling features alive globally without having to
+rewrite them.
+
+Furthermore, this architectural shift eliminates the need for several complex workarounds currently required in MultiKueue:
+
+- It removes the need for `MultiKueueOrchestratedPreemption` and its associated complexities.
+- It eliminates the need to declare higher, inflated quotas in the manager compared to the workers.
+- It removes the need to maintain a multiplier in the manager quota to account for fleet-wide capacity.
+
+### User Stories (Optional)
+
+#### Story 1: Global Fair Sharing
+
+As a platform administrator managing a fleet of GPU clusters across multiple
+regions, I want to define team quotas centrally on the manager cluster. If Team
+A is under quota and Team B is over quota, I want the manager to ensure Team A
+can preempt Team B's workloads globally, regardless of which specific worker
+cluster Team B's workloads happen to be running on.
+
+#### Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption
```

  > As a platform administrator, I can define quotas for all worker clusters in the manager cluster
  > without having to decide how those quotas should be distributed between individual clusters.
  
  @mukund-wayve I'm trying to understand how centralized TAS enables this. Have you already tried doing this with the current MultiKueue, and did you find any problems with it until preemption is required?

+# KEP-13345: Support centralized scheduling and quota management in MultiKueue
+
+<!-- toc -->
+- [Summary](#summary)
+- [Motivation](#motivation)
+  - [Goals](#goals)
+  - [Non-Goals](#non-goals)
+- [Proposal](#proposal)
+  - [User Stories (Optional)](#user-stories-optional)
+    - [Story 1: Global Fair Sharing](#story-1-global-fair-sharing)
+    - [Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption](#story-2-cross-cluster-preemption)
+  - [Risks and Mitigations](#risks-and-mitigations)
+- [Drawbacks](#drawbacks)
+- [Design Details](#design-details)
+  - [Remote Inventory Sync](#remote-inventory-sync)
+  - [Manager-Authoritative Placement](#manager-authoritative-placement)
+  - [Worker Pass-Through](#worker-pass-through)
+  - [Test Plan](#test-plan)
+  - [Graduation Criteria](#graduation-criteria)
+- [Alternatives](#alternatives)
+<!-- /toc -->
+
+## Summary
+
+This KEP proposes an option to use the MultiKueue manager as a central scheduler
+across all worker clusters, specifically leveraging Topology Aware Scheduling
+(TAS). In this model, worker clusters act simply as compute pools. The central
+manager handles all admission, preemption, and fair-sharing decisions, and all
+project or team-specific quotas are managed entirely on the manager cluster.
+
+This new centralized scheduling scheme is necessarily more computationally
+expensive than MultiKueue's current mode which primarily manages global quota
+and dispatch and relies on workers to make authoritative scheduling decisions.
+Users will be able to select which mode a MultiKueue instance uses for their
+scheduling and scaling requirements.
+
+## Motivation
+
+Currently, MultiKueue delegates final scheduling decisions to the worker
+clusters. While this works well for many setups, it makes it difficult to
+achieve strict global fair sharing and cross-cluster preemption across an entire
+fleet because the manager lacks visibility into the actual physical capacity and
+topology of the worker nodes.
+
+Allowing the central manager to make these decisions helps organizations that
+manage compute capacity across multiple regions or clouds to better optimize
+their resources. It also simplifies cluster administration by consolidating team
+quota management into a single control plane, rather than needing to manage and
+sync quotas across every worker cluster.
+
+### Goals
+
+- Enable the MultiKueue manager to act as a central scheduler across all worker clusters.
+- Support all workloads currently supported by MultiKueue.
+- Ensure global preemption and fair sharing work seamlessly across the fleet.
+- The manager computes the exact node-level placement (e.g., `kubernetes.io/hostname`) for workloads.
+- Workers execute the manager's placement verbatim without re-evaluating TAS.
+
+### Non-Goals
+
+- Supporting workloads that span multiple clusters (cross-cluster gangs). A
+  single workload must still fit entirely within one worker cluster.
+- Supporting dynamic node provisioning (autoscaling) for TAS workloads in this iteration.
+- Fundamentally change the existing MultiKueue design.
+
+## Proposal
+
+We propose extending MultiKueue to allow the manager to ingest physical node and
+pod inventory from worker clusters into its own scheduler TAS cache. When a
+workload is admitted, the manager will compute the full `TopologyAssignment`
+(treating the worker cluster as the top-level topology domain) and project a
+host-exact placement onto the remote workload. The worker cluster will then skip
+its own scheduling cycle and apply the manager's placement directly.
+
+This solution works because all workloads are already mirrored in the manager
+via MultiKueue. The reason global features like fair-sharing and cross-cluster
+preemption don't work correctly today is simply because the manager lacks
+information about the physical capacity of the clusters and the specific
+topology placement of the workloads. By centralizing the TAS cache across all
+clusters, we give the manager the missing physical context it needs, bringing
+all of these advanced scheduling features alive globally without having to
+rewrite them.
+
+Furthermore, this architectural shift eliminates the need for several complex workarounds currently required in MultiKueue:
+
+- It removes the need for `MultiKueueOrchestratedPreemption` and its associated complexities.
+- It eliminates the need to declare higher, inflated quotas in the manager compared to the workers.
+- It removes the need to maintain a multiplier in the manager quota to account for fleet-wide capacity.
+
+### User Stories (Optional)
+
+#### Story 1: Global Fair Sharing
+
+As a platform administrator managing a fleet of GPU clusters across multiple
+regions, I want to define team quotas centrally on the manager cluster. If Team
+A is under quota and Team B is over quota, I want the manager to ensure Team A
+can preempt Team B's workloads globally, regardless of which specific worker
+cluster Team B's workloads happen to be running on.
+
+#### Story 2: Cross-Cluster Preemption
+
+As a machine learning engineer, I submit a high-priority distributed training
+job that requires a specific network topology (e.g., all pods on the same rack).
+If the fleet is full, I want the central manager to find the optimal worker
+cluster and preempt exactly the right lower-priority workloads on that specific
+cluster to make room for my job's topology requirements.
+
+### Risks and Mitigations
+
+**Risk:** Scalability and performance bottlenecks on the manager cluster.
+**Mitigation:** The manager's TAS cache does not store full pod specs. It only
+caches a highly optimized, pre-aggregated counter of the resources used by
+non-TAS pods per node. This ensures the memory footprint and CPU overhead remain
+minimal even at large scale.
+
+**Risk:** Worker cluster disconnection.
+**Mitigation:** The system relies on MultiKueue's existing `workerLostTimeout`
+mechanism. If a worker disconnects, the manager waits for a grace period. If the
+worker does not reconnect, the manager evicts the workload and retries
+scheduling it on a healthy cluster. When a worker reconnects, the manager
+performs a fresh `List` to rebuild the TAS cache for that cluster.
```
